42,2026-10-08,"The Internet, and large groups of people in general, tend to have a remarkably short collective memory. Whether it’s the nature of algorithms or the people themselves to always be chasing the next novelty, maintaining long-term engagement for even relatively small changes can be challenging. Distractions abound, and motivation tends to fade over time. This past spring, Discord’s unpopular decision to force age verification resulted in a brief flash-in-the-pan of outrage that dissipated when they promised to delay its roll-out. Rather than backtrack on unpopular policies, they’ve been slowly boiling the frog with incremental changes towards this same goal in the meantime. One of the options to deal with this is to migrate over to something like a Matrix server, which has a number of benefits over Discord including being open-source and self-hostable.
Unlike Discord, a closed platform subject to the whims of a private company, Matrix is a communications protocol and open standard. Its major server and client implementations are open source, so anyone can build tools around it and integrate it with other systems that also use this protocol. This protocol-vs-platform comparison mirrors email, which is another protocol that isn’t locked down by any one company, and which can easily be used to send messages across various providers. The most commonly used server-side software for Matrix is called Synapse and the most prominent general-purpose clients are the various implementations of Element, and my exploration uses these defaults.

One of the problems with choosing a Discord replacement for one’s family, friends, or online gaming community is that there are a huge number of options. In much the same way Linux itself fails to make a dent in the desktop operating system market, open-source and community-driven projects are relatively easy to code and release but difficult to market and build up a sustainable userbase. So, while there are platforms like Stoat, XMPP, and others which provide self-hosted text and video communication, many of these services have a very small userbase or have some other shortcoming, and on some deeper level it can feel like flipping a coin to choose between any one of them and their myriad of forks.
For me personally, my choice of Matrix over any other option was partially driven by the fact that it seems to have an active and helpful userbase compared to others, it has it has strong end-to-end encryption support which is generally enabled by default (for private chats), and it’s possible to host on computers that I own. Another big reason I picked Matrix to experiment with is that self-hosting Matrix is optional. There is a large and active server called matrix.org that functions as a default server of sorts, which allows almost anyone to sign up and start chatting without having to install anything other than a Matrix client like Element. In this way, matrix.org feels a lot like IRC in its golden age because these large servers allow mostly anonymous Internet users to communicate about various topics in dedicated chatrooms without any great effort.
Matrix is often loosely lumped in with other “federated” social media like Lemmy or Mastodon because, like these ActivityPub-based systems, there is no central server like Discord or Reddit and no overarching authority policing the entire network. So a Matrix server can federate with other Matrix servers, allowing users on the two servers to participate in the same rooms and exchange messages. If one server goes offline or decides to defederate for whatever reason, that doesn’t bring down the rest of the network, although users and rooms hosted exclusively on that server will of course be affected. Federation is also optional, and independent servers for one’s own family or friends that don’t federate with any other server is just as possible as a large server federating with all of the major servers like matrix.org. Compared to Discord, this decentralized structure makes it much more robust to a single entity’s whims.
Although a different protocol from ActivityPub which is used for other federated social media, Matrix also has a bridging network that allow it to communicate with other platforms, including IRC and Discord, as well as numerous other messaging services. So, in theory, setting up a Matrix server doesn’t preclude anyone’s friends on Discord from communicating with them, and might make it easier to migrate a friend group to the more open platform over a period of time, rather than expecting everyone to pick up and move all at once.
To that end, setting up a Matrix server does take a little more effort than creating a channel in Discord. I have a Proxmox server that makes setting up new servers relatively easy, but the server- and client-side software runs just fine on most Linux distributions without the help of virtualization. To start, I spun up an LXC container to run Synapse which is easy enough, but only includes text and photo chatrooms on its own. I was able to make this available on the wider Internet using a Cloudflare tunnel at first. With the Cloudflare URL, encryption keys, and the Element clients on various machines owned by my family and friends, everyone was easily able to get in to the chat rooms and communicate, with full end-to-end encryption and with messages stored on a machine I physically control. If you’re following along and want to stop here with just text-based chat, that’s certainly a viable and simpler option.
However, this does fall short of matching Discord’s quick voice and video calling, and this is where Matrix server administration can start to get a bit cumbersome. The short story here is that, at the time I set up my servers, there was a legacy calling mode alongside a newer one, so to support both there were two additional servers that needed to be set up. The legacy mode needs a TURN server, and the modern voice and video calling system supported by Element generally needs a LiveKit server. Since I set up my servers, though, this architecture has been simplified with TURN becoming less central to typical Matrix servers.
The next hurdle for me was getting around my Internet service provider’s network address translation (NAT). This is part of the reason I started using a Cloudflare tunnel in the first place, but the free tier is basically only an HTTP/WebSocket proxy which has no way to accept the UDP connectivity that TURN and LiveKit normally require for media. To solve that problem I turned to a small VPS that I rent which has a public-facing IP address. (I actually put an OpenOSCAR server on here first, back when this project got popular, to test out its capabilities and get it ready for my Matrix server.) The TURN server is lightweight enough to run on the VPS directly, but LiveKit is much more resource-intensive than I’m willing to pay for. This means LiveKit has to run on my server, so I set up a WireGuard connection from the VPS back to its container on the Proxmox machine.
Part of what makes this setup a little more difficult than average, besides the NAT-induced headaches, is that even once these packets get to my router, which is also hosted on my Proxmox machine, they go to a DMZ on a separate VLAN in my home network so that public-facing traffic can never get through to the rest of my LAN. But that DMZ includes a number of other public-facing services which are managed by an nginx server, adding yet another layer to this onion of a system. So between the support services for the Matrix server, the network layers, the off-site VPS, and the Matrix server itself, my setup involves six virtual machines, some interaction with Cloudflare, and one additional (albeit relatively inexpensive) paid service. This also doesn’t count the services I run on the Proxmox host and in other VMs to monitor web traffic and improve network security.
Even this setup, once I spent a considerable amount of time troubleshooting, has its downsides. For some reason which still eludes me, users on iPhones running the Element X client are randomly unable to send picture messages to chats. Occasionally a service on one of my many VMs will fail and need maintenance, and this seems to only happen when I’m not near my laptop. But perhaps most notably is that my Matrix server still doesn’t federate with any others. This isn’t really a problem as much as it was a conscious choice, though; it’s something that’s possible with a public endpont of some sort, so it’s my VPS makes this an option for me, but I haven’t attempted it yet. Mostly this is because the people on my Matrix server that actively use it are family members who don’t need or want that kind of exposure to the deep Internet, and my broader experience on matrix.org was often marred by things like crypto scams and generally desolate chatrooms.
If you’re still with us after that extremely convoluted journey, it might seem like this is a lot to ask to replace a free service that’s been fairly instrumental in making it easy for gamers to interact with each other over the Internet and which has also revolutionized online chatrooms (for better or worse). But they have demonstrated time after time that they’re willing to slowly erode user trust, privacy, security, and experience in favor of shareholder value, and as they start rolling out “features” like age and identity verification they introduce more categories of sensitive information that are stored somewhere, and more potential points of failure. Given the long history of major companies failing to protect user data, it seems inevitable that someone will eventually make a mistake and expose information that users never expected or wanted Discord to collect in the first place. So for those who still have intact memories of Discord’s prior attempts to implement extremely unpopular policies, or even memories of other companies who have failed to protect sensitive user information in the past, a Matrix server might be worth the attempt.
You can’t convince a friend groups of non nerds to suddenly take an interest in migrating to the neckbeard land of Linux users with poor taste in good UI/UX, and expect them to also become infrastructure engineers to maintain their social lives. It’s just nigh impossible to budge a crowd of non technical people into doing anything. A lot of my old groups wouldn’t move off Telegram for Signal “because I can’t import my stickers/use my exiting packs” (this was before Signal had even added stickers mind you). People will not migrate until Open Source fixes the UI/UX problem that apps like Discord have annoyingly, solved through chucking money, Tencent, and Peter Tiel at the problem.
Keep in mind that in many countries simply having Signal installed on your phone can get you into serious trouble (prosecuted for being spy or anti-government). If you don’t want to end up in a cell with thief or narcoman just after you arrived better stick to standard apps.
btw. the so-called “burner phone” approach is a major red flag too and you might be arrested.
Signal was the example. Not saying it’s the right place to go; it sure isn’t. No Matrix, Stoat, or whatever hot new thing is going to replace Discord until people can figure out how to make federation seamless (or non existent), and build a UI and UX that won’t feel archaic. Federation also is kinda a pretty meh idea too. Take for example, a situation where two server admins argue and defederate – you suddenly lose contact with you friends on the other side. Looking at you, Mastodon & the fediverse. Generally if a social media platform loses your ability to be social, it has failed as a platform.
Same thing with twitter and its alternatives. People wanted to leave, but found that the alternatives didn’t have the network effect as strongly, and if you slipped up and accidentally said you only supported the current deranged hysteria 105% instead of 115%, they would dox you and harass you and your extended family and pets IRL for three solid years.
So people went back and dealt with the “trolls” and “bots” (people they don’t agree with)
The only winning move is not to play. I know not everyone can be privileged to do that. If I had such a choice with Discord, I’d absolutely opt for it. But for social media.. Xitter, Bluesky, Instagram, TikTok and the likes.. yeah I don’t need them in my life and mental health has been better for it :)

  they would dox you and harass you and your extended family and pets IRL for three solid years

This also describes what it’s like being a queer/trans/lgbtq+ person on the public internet pretty well. You don’t even need to be provocative; you just need to exist! ._.
Oh, it’s just some boring article about social media or messaging apps.
First glance at the picture and I thought it would be “If You Can’t Say Anything Nice 2: Electric Boogaloo”. So disappointed.
Discord is frequently use in hacker/maker communities. The article is highly relevant.
You’re probably too young to remember the tsunami of fecal matter that hit the comments section back then. Man, seeing that I was never happier to like and subscribe.
Which reminds me, had still on discord? If so, change of scenery planned already?
Yep, you won’t convince the whole 40,728 members of the Voron Design community to up and go that easily. They’re still trying to move off Reddit for serializations and have been for as long as I’ve been a participant – which is about 4.5 years.
@Hackaday staff : this is the correct link to Synapse project (the link in the article point to a former repository which is now read-only) : https://github.com/element-hq/synapse

Been running a matrix server for our small hackerspace for about 5 years now using the MASH ansible notebook: https://github.com/spantaleev/matrix-docker-ansible-deploy
I am not really familiar with docker so I have a dedicated (virtual) machine for the matrix server, and the ansible script manages everything.
It took an afternoon to get set up and about an hour of maintenance annually to pull the updates. I really couldn’t be happier.
But the most under appreciated part is how Matix supports ‘bridges’. The server is set up so that you can bridge to all of your non-federated chat (including Discord) into the matrix and not have to deal with all of the anti-patterns associated with those services. Just a single interface with all of your chats, like the lord intended.
I’ve wondered about this.
Could you have a Discord – > matrix bridge that relays chat back and forth, make it some sort of admin that is the only one allowed to that.
It means users could move over slowly and no one misses anything.
The message could not be decoded.
Matrix E2EE really sucks. I don’t understand the need to force everything to this BYOK strategy.  Use it only if you think that https isn’t enough to protect from your goverment.
And lets face it; Matrix is just a bridge central server – allowing WhatsApp/Telegram to continue as normal. It might even make everything worse as there is no real push for anyone leaving users closed corporate platforms.
Tailscale could have helped as well.
I do actually use Tailscale for some things, but one of my goals was to make it as easy as possible for new users to join my Matrix server. Saying “hey, you need to install Element, here are your keys, and also now install this OTHER thing, and make sure it’s running when you want to chat” kind of makes most peoples’ eyes glaze over.
Actually, that would make me tell the friend with the Matrix server to talk to me through e-mail instead.  If he cannot produce a configuration where I simply install a client and access the server through it, well,  said software won´t receive much attention
I’ve been on the lookout for a Discord alternative for years, and Matrix really isn’t good enough to carry the torch. It has so many bugs and deal-breaking issues that it straight up makes me mad every time I use it. For an (open-source!) alternative that competently copies Discord, Fluxer is worth taking a look instead. Chances are, Fluxer is what most people looking to get off Discord want, where if they care more about things like e2e encryption, Signal and co. remain viable choices. I use both.

        Gartner predicts more than half will fold by 2030, even as businesses begin testing the technology's usefulness
    
More than half of quantum computing startups will be out of business by 2030, Gartner predicts, as an overcrowded market struggles to generate enough commercial revenue.
The analyst expects customers to reject technologies that fail to achieve fault-tolerant quantum computing – reliable computation despite errors in the underlying hardware.
Despite that forecast, Gartner says enterprises are making initial investments as quantum technologies begin to show early signs of practical business advantage.
Broad commercial usefulness remains elusive, but organizations are building expertise rather than waiting for the technology to mature.
""CIOs and IT leaders should treat their quantum journey as a multi-year endeavor with the aim of gaining a competitive business advantage over their closest competitors,"" says Gaurav Gupta, Gartner VP analyst and chief of research for its emerging market dynamics team.
Estimates put the number of quantum startups at between 200 and 300, competing in a market Gartner forecasts will generate $1.1 billion in worldwide revenue in 2027. That is a tiny fraction of the quarterly revenue of companies such as Samsung and Nvidia, but still up on the $869.9 million forecast for this year.
As The Register reported a couple of years back, developing useful quantum computers requires sustained funding, and while big players like IBM or Google have no shortage of revenue streams, startups may be subsisting on seed funding that will eventually run out if they can't persuade investors that they are onto something.
The quantum industry therefore still faces numerous challenges, but continues to make steady progress, not just in the number of quantum bits (qubits) in a system, but in algorithm development, gate speeds, and reliability.
And breakthroughs are happening more rapidly than initially expected, at least according to Gartner.
""The early quantum advantage era has arrived because noisy intermediate-scale quantum processors now have enough qubits to do limited complex tasks with improved error reduction,"" claims Gupta.
The firm identifies the public sector and the financial industry as the two markets likely to spend the most on quantum computing initiatives.
Of these, the public sector is forecast to lead at first, driven by a strong desire for nations to gain technical leadership. Total spend here in 2027 is expected to amount to $245 million, up from $219 million this year.
Banking, finance, and insurance firms are expected to overtake the public sector in 2028, spending $289 million against its $280 million. Gartner forecasts that they will remain the largest customer sector through 2030.
The US Department of Energy recently kicked off an initiative that will see up to ten participants compete to deliver a fault-tolerant, scientifically relevant quantum computer by 2028, an ambitious goal that is almost certain to fail given the modest amount of funding it is offering. ®

Tech > Computing > Tablets
The new tablets tie together the Amazon media ecosystem so you can pick up where you left off from just about anywhere.
Writer
Writer
October 8, 2026, 11:12 am ET
A new lineup of Amazon tablets has been announced, and the devices are designed for both work and play. Amazon Alexa Tablets combine cloud-based device syncing functionality, onboard AI capabilities and the entirety of Amazon’s media catalog, making them something akin to on-the-go entertainment hubs.
Each of the three Alexa Tablet models has an aluminum chassis, which will protect what’ll be some of the thinnest devices Amazon has ever released. More importantly, Fire OS and the Amazon Appstore are going away. Instead of a custom, forked operating system, the Alexa Tablets will run Android 16 at launch, and will upgrade to Android 17 in early 2027. That means these devices will offer full Play Store functionality, giving you access to plenty of apps that haven’t previously been compatible with Amazon tablets.
But the software capabilities are what I suspect will be the real draw. If you use an Amazon account on your computer, phone, TV or other device, it will automatically sync up with the Alexa Tablets. A dynamic tab on the home screen allows you to quickly pick up movies, shows, books and games wherever you left off. There’s also an algorithmic “For You” layout that shows you other content you might enjoy based off of what you’re currently enjoying, like a streaming model that pulls together every form of media in one place.
More from CNET
Avid readers will be able to access their Audible and Kindle libraries in one place, and a new Kindle mode also silences notifications, turns down blue light and enhances battery life. I got a firsthand look at the e-ink and color paper modes at an in-person event in New York and was impressed with how the pages looked on-screen. The texture of the display is sufficiently papery, too. (TCL’s Nxtpaper devices may soon be given a run for their money.)
Each Alexa Tablet is deeply integrated with Alexa Plus AI, allowing you to converse with it and pull answers from Amazon’s Silk browser on the fly. Opting into on-screen intelligence allows the generative model to see what’s on the display. It can answer questions about images in a browser, locate listings for specific products shown in a social media video and more. During the product demonstration, Alexa Plus took upwards of half a minute to respond to complicated prompts, but the onboard model responded accurately to verbal requests about managing a personal calendar and finding nearby restaurants based on dietary restrictions.
Kevin Keith, vice president of Amazon’s devices team, told me that the new lineup of Amazon Alexa Tablets will supersede the Fire Tablets entirely. The company will wind down production of its old product and sell the remaining stock over the next few months. Once those Fire tablets sell out, Amazon has committed to providing at least four years of software support, including security and operating system updates.
The cloud syncing, Kindle mode and Alexa Plus on-screen intelligence features are packaged into every Alexa Tablet, but these devices aren’t all built the same. The three models have different hardware and peripheral capabilities. Here’s what you can expect from the first wave of Alexa Tablets.
Amazon’s new flagship tablet feels like a true jack of all trades. It’s an entertainment hub, an ereader and a personal AI assistant that can be converted into a work laptop using a few simple peripherals.
The most premium configuration has a whopping 16GB of memory, which will help with a more demanding workload for folks who routinely use the device’s multiwindowing capabilities or more complex Play Store applications.
This feels like the platonic ideal of a hybrid device. It’s more portable than a laptop, but has all your favorite mobile apps. It’s powerful enough for some light gaming but you can use it to read a new book, too. It’s not specialized for any particular task, but it can do a bit of everything.  Its launch price is also cheaper than most budget laptops to boot.
This middle-of-the-road Alexa Tablet is likely for people who want the entertainment hub functionality of the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro but are willing to make some compromises to save some money.
The 11-inch model is still able to use a keyboard, but it doesn’t have a physical pogo pin connection. You can still use the stylus to draw on-screen, but there’s less space to do so. More importantly, you’re losing out on the higher storage options available on the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro.
This might not be a portable workstation, but it’s a solid entertainment hub nonetheless: You still get high resolution and all of Amazon’s media is in one place. If you’re looking for a better bargain on a “fun” tablet, the Alexa Tablet 11 could end up being the perfect middle-ground for you.
The diminutive Alexa Tablet 8 has the same built-in device cloud syncing and Alexa Plus capabilities as the rest of the lineup, but I wouldn’t expect this model to serve as an entertainment hub or work device.
Even though it has full Play Store access, it has less memory and storage space than the more expensive Alexa Tablets. I reckon most people will use this 8.7-inch tablet as a pocket-size ereader in the new and improved Kindle mode, with its additional capabilities serving as a bonus.
Just like the regular Fire Tablets, the Fire Kids lineup is going the way of the dodo. But it’s being replaced with two Alexa Tablets that are also built specifically for children. Keith told me the design philosophy for these tablets was to bring them more in line with devices for adults while maintaining safety features and parental controls.
“We know kids want a tablet, not a toy, so we add the same hardware as the Alexa Tablet,” he said. “We ship it with a bumper, a completely different UI and software built just for kids.”
Younger children will benefit from the new 8.7-inch tablet, which has a soft, foam cover around the chassis, while a sleeker 11-inch model will come with a more form-fitting, kid-proof case.
The devices come with the generative-AI Storybuilder tool, which lets kids verbalize stories and see custom visuals for them. This is paired off with its own set of parental controls and a parental dashboard that provides weekly insights on tablet usage. Every Amazon Kids tablet comes with a one-year subscription to Amazon Kids Plus.
The full lineup of Amazon Alexa Tablets is available for preorder now, and will begin shipping out across the US on Oct. 14.

47,2026-10-08,"Microsoft has just unveiled the most powerful laptop in its Surface range, and it's set to take on Apple's popular MacBook Pro for demanding work applications. If you're in the market for a higher-end machine, there's a couple of things in this premium option worth learning about – including a brand new processor that no other laptop on the market has yet.
The Surface Laptop Ultra runs on an RTX Spark N1X chip, which is basically all-in-one silicon from Nvidia that combines the company's Blackwell graphics architecture (seen in the current-gen 50-series GPUs), up to 20 cores for the Arm-based CPU, and up to 128GB of unified memory.
Microsoft says that'll sort you out when running AI models locally, working on 3D modeling and video editing projects, and gaming too.
One thing to note about this PC is that it's running on an Arm-based processor, which some apps don't directly support. You can still run pretty much any Windows software on it using the Prism emulation layer, but they may not perform as well as on Intel and AMD's x86-64 chips.
You'll also find a bright and color-accurate 15-inch touchscreen, loads of ports, a magnetic USB-C port for the power adapter, and 2.5 times the thermal capacity of the lower-end Surface Laptop (the amount of heat its cooling can remove) – all in a chassis less than 18 mm thick, with 15 hours of video playback time from the battery.
The Ultra starts at US$2,599.99, and be configured with more cores and memory. Microsoft says this will square up formidably against a 14-inch MacBook Pro, which comes in a powerful configuration with an M5 Pro chip (18-core CPU, 20-core GPU), 24GB RAM, and 1TB storage for $2,499. The Ultra said to beat it hollow on benchmarks for AI tasks, including image and video generation.
That said, Apple already has a loyal audience for its MacOS-based performance laptops, and is way better at marketing and distributing MacBook Pros than Microsoft. The latter likely won't move a lot of Ultras, but it is trying to fire a warning shot across Apple's bow to signal that it's serious about enabling high-end performance for AI apps and services on Windows machines.
I'm more interested in what Microsoft's doing to make the Ultra easier to live with over years. The laptop's components aren't glued into place, so it'll be easier to take the whole thing apart, and carry out repairs. The company says it'll publish maintenance guides and make replacement parts available to help with that, and you can also upgrade its storage to 2TB down the line.
If this sounds familiar, it might be because we've previously covered hardware brand Framework, which designs its laptops for repairability from the ground up. Microsoft's effort doesn't come close – the Ultra doesn't support plug-and-play modules to add ports and card readers, and allow for upgrades using a single tool that comes in the box) – but it's a step in the right direction. I sure would like to see more brands follow suit with future models.
We'll have to wait just a wee bit to see if the Ultra has what it takes to outshine the beloved MacBook Pro in real-world testing. It's up for pre-order now, and deliveries begin shipping October 16. Find the Surface Laptop Ultra on Microsoft's site.

49,2026-10-08,"Mike Savage didn't let a modest salary or limited savings keep him from buying real estate.
He cycled through sales roles in his 20s before becoming a firefighter and EMT. His wife worked as a teacher. There was ""not a whole lot of money between us,"" he told Business Insider.
Still, the couple managed to buy their first home in Massachusetts in 2010 using a USDA rural housing loan, which allowed for a 0% down payment, and an $8,000 first-time homebuyer credit available at the time amid the housing crisis. 
When work brought them to the South about three years later, they converted that home into a rental and bought another primary residence in Mount Pleasant, South Carolina.
As their family grew, Savage said, the couple began to question whether their combined incomes would be enough to cover education costs, save for retirement, and build the life they wanted. After buying a few primary residences, they began looking to rental real estate as a way to build wealth beyond their day jobs.
Savage's first investment property was a $49,000, three-bedroom house in Charleston, South Carolina, which he bought in 2015.
He and his wife had already tapped retirement funds to help pay for their move to Charleston and had ""nothing in the bank,"" he said. But Savage owned his truck outright, so he took out a loan against it to cover the roughly $10,000 down payment.
Borrowing against an asset, such as a paid-off vehicle, allows an owner to use it as collateral for a loan. It can unlock cash quickly, but it also puts the vehicle at risk if the borrower can't make payments.
For Savage, the loan offered a way into the market without waiting years to save up a cash down payment. 
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
His mindset at the time, he said, was: ""What is there to lose? This is what I want to do. I just have to figure out how to make it happen.""
Savage later tapped the equity in his primary residence to continue growing his real-estate holdings.
He and his wife took out a roughly $30,000 home equity line of credit, or HELOC, against their Mount Pleasant home and used it to help buy a $250,000 townhouse in downtown Charleston. They bought the townhouse as a primary residence, kept the Mount Pleasant home, and converted it into a short-term rental.
A HELOC lets homeowners borrow against their home equity, offering a revolving line of credit they can tap as needed during a set draw period.
""With our incomes and what we were making, it was always going to be difficult to qualify for big loans and get people to give us money,"" Savage said. The HELOC gave the couple another source of capital.
They then repeated the process, taking out a HELOC against the downtown property to buy the home where they now live. They converted the downtown home into a short-term rental, too. It performed well, he said, until Charleston changed its short-term rental rules about a year later, forcing them to switch to a long-term rental.
Around 2018, looking to take his portfolio to the next level, Savage began attending local real-estate groups and investor meetups. He learned that investors don't always need a 20% down payment for every deal. With a broader understanding of financing options, including hard-money lending and the BRRRR method, his acquisitions accelerated: He bought three long-term rentals in 2020 and six more in 2021.
Business Insider reviewed property-tax records confirming Savage's ownership of several properties.
Savage hadn't originally planned to quit his job, but when the fire department changed its pay structure and reduced some of his compensation, he began to question how much control he really had over his family's financial future.
""I remember feeling very helpless,"" he said. ""I can't rely on these employers to provide for myself and my family.""
Until then, Savage had viewed real estate mainly as a way to supplement his retirement savings. The pay change pushed him to consider it as a career — and to cofound SynergyStays, a short-term rental revenue management business.
The business grew out of his own experience as an Airbnb host. He had worked with several property-management companies, he said, but even the best ones left him feeling that his rentals were being managed ""less as a business, and more as, well, a property.""  
As SynergyStays has grown, Savage has also shifted his investment strategy from accumulating as many units as possible to maximizing the income from each unit. This year, he sold four long-term rentals, paid down some debt, and said he had about $450,000 in cash from the sales. He plans to put roughly $350,000 toward a single short-term rental that he believes could generate more income than the four properties combined.
""I want to achieve more through less,"" he said.
Savage estimates his net worth at about $2 million. In his final full year with the fire department, 2021, he said his take-home pay was about $40,000. The following year, a single rental — an accessory dwelling unit he built in his backyard — generated almost the same amount: $35,000 in gross income.
With income from rentals and SynergyStays — which he said each accounts for roughly half of his total income — Savage felt comfortable leaving the department.
His version of financial independence doesn't mean he no longer works. ""I do have to work a lot right now, and I have to work hard,"" he said, but he no longer depends on an employer to decide his pay, schedule, or time off. ""I get to decide that. I go out and make decisions, and work harder, or do things smarter to help myself make more money.""

50,2026-10-08,"Soda Labs’ GC-MPC system lets banks and payment companies compute on private data without leaving public blockchains. NextBlock, a Luxembourg venture firm, funded the entire seed round.
Pieter van Poecke, founder and general partner of NextBlock
NextBlock is putting $3 million behind an increasingly important challenge for public blockchains: how to give financial institutions privacy without forcing them to move their activity onto separate, purpose-built networks.
The Luxembourg-based venture capital firm has funded Soda Labs’ entire seed round. It is backing the company’s programmable privacy infrastructure for financial activity on public blockchains.
Soda Labs has spent the past two and a half years developing a cryptographic privacy system. It combines garbled circuits with multiparty computation, or GC-MPC. It relies on established cryptography including AES and SHA256 and runs on ordinary cloud CPUs rather than specialized hardware.
The technology is designed to enable private computation while applications remain connected to existing public blockchain infrastructure. That addresses a fundamental problem for banks, payment companies and other financial institutions. Public blockchains may offer liquidity and users, but financial data cannot always be visible to everyone.
“What attracted us to Soda was not only the underlying cryptography, but the practicality of the technology for the financial workloads we believe will matter most onchain,” said Pieter van Poecke, Founder and General Partner of NextBlock. “Soda already had a working product and paying customers. We believe its differentiated technical IP, deeply technical founding team and strong commercial instincts give the company a compelling foundation for its next phase.”
Soda’s technology has already processed more than 100 million transactions on the COTI network. There, it supports applications including tokenization platform Zoniqx and perpetuals exchange PriveX. PriveX has processed more than $20 billion in trading volume. Zoniqx is onboarding issuers across multiple asset classes and jurisdictions.
Soda is now extending its architecture beyond gcEVM, its Ethereum Layer 2 privacy layer that remains live on COTI. The next step is Soda Bubble, a chain-agnostic coprocessor for programmable privacy.
Bubble is designed to process developer-defined workloads originating from different blockchains. It does so without exposing the underlying private data publicly or to Soda Labs itself.
The company is expanding the system across major EVM ecosystems including Ethereum, Polygon, Arbitrum and Base. Expansion to non-EVM networks, including Solana, is also underway.
Its Bubble Validator Network enables participants to mathematically verify that computations involving private data have been performed faithfully.
For NextBlock, the investment reflects a focus on infrastructure that could make privacy-preserving computation practical for real financial applications. It does so without removing that activity from existing public blockchain ecosystems.
Soda Labs expects to publish updated performance benchmarks in the coming weeks based on testing conducted on Arbitrum.
The company says the tests measure the complete transaction lifecycle on the live network. That includes encryption, MPC computation, consensus and settlement. They do not measure the cryptographic operation in isolation.
According to Soda, the results represent a five- to tenfold improvement over its previously published benchmark.
The company also reports that its garbled-circuit MPC architecture can deliver approximately 10 to 100 times greater throughput and 100 to 1,000 times lower cost per transaction. That is compared with currently available alternatives, while operating on standard cloud CPUs. Soda has not yet publicly released the new benchmark results.
“Public blockchains already have the liquidity, users and financial applications. What they lack is a way for regulated money to move without showing everyone everything,” said Avishay Yanai, Co-Founder and CEO of Soda Labs. “Bubble gives banks, payment companies and tokenization platforms privacy with controlled disclosure, on the chains they already use. This round lets us take it from pilots to production.”
The $3 million will support Soda Labs’ go-to-market strategy, expansion of its validator network and blockchain coverage, and team growth. It will also fund integrations with banks, payment companies, tokenization platforms and other financial infrastructure providers.
Soda is also working with financial and infrastructure organizations on several undisclosed pilots, with the objective of converting them into production deployments.
Avishay Yanai, CEO and PhD in cryptography, and Meital Levy, CTO and PhD in algorithms, founded the company. Together they bring more than 20 years of experience in security.
For NextBlock, the investment puts capital behind a simple thesis: public blockchains already have the financial activity. The next challenge is making that activity private enough for regulated finance.

51,2026-10-08,"Apple released iOS 27 to everyone a few weeks ago, and it includes several CarPlay changes worth checking out. Here are a few changes I’ve been loving so far.
9to5Mac is supported by Sony: Sony is running a massive sale on its best BRAVIA TVs, ranging from its entry-level to flagship options — shop now to save!

Siri AI
Siri AI is the flagship feature of iOS 27, and it extends to CarPlay. In fact, I’ve found it to be especially useful via CarPlay. Siri AI can answer questions around your personal context, world knowledge, and much more in iOS 27.
For example, you can ask Siri to find a specific address or location in your email or text messages, then begin routing to that location, all while using just your voice.
Robust, hands-free control is important in the car, and iOS 27 finally gives Siri the power that it deserves in CarPlay.
Now Playing changes
CarPlay in iOS 27 offers a persistent “Now Playing” MiniPlayer throughout audio apps. This appears in the upper-right corner of the interface and shows playback controls and album artwork.
The Now Playing interface in CarPlay has also been updated to include an audio scrubber at long last. You can drag the scrubber to easily skip to different parts of a song or podcast without using steering wheel controls or reaching for your iPhone.
New wallpapers
CarPlay isn’t known for its robust collection of wallpapers, and Apple doesn’t let you use your own photos for some reason.
In iOS 27, Apple has at least added a collection of new wallpapers that you can choose from. They are similar to the stock iOS 27 wallpapers available on iPhone.
Stability improvements
iOS 27 focuses a lot on platform improvements to performance and reliability, and that applies to CarPlay too. Apple says iOS 27 addresses two key things in CarPlay:
So if you’ve ever had problems with those two things in CarPlay, it’s possible that iOS 27 makes the experience much better.
Coming soon(ish): Video app support
Finally, Apple says that developers can now make dedicated video apps for CarPlay in iOS 27. The feature, however, requires support from automakers. This means it might be a while before you can actually use any video apps with CarPlay in your vehicle.
While the dream for many people is still CarPlay Ultra, it’s really nice to see Apple make meaningful improvements to the version of CarPlay already available in millions of cars on the road today.

David Zaslav, ex-CEO of the former Warner Bros Discovery, saw a windfall of just over $600 million as he handed over his WBD shares to Paramount at $31 each.

	An SEC filing Thursday shows the October 6 exchange of tranches of common stock and options as Paramount closed its acquisition of WBD and changed its name to Skydance. The merger, unveiled in February and valued at $110 billion, called for Paramount to acquire all of WBD’s outstanding shares at $31. Zaslav’s stock options vested immediately as the deal closed.

	Executives and board members of WBD are required to register the stock sales and the numbers are high. Zaslav and the Warner board drove a hard bargain, rejecting a series of takeover offers by Paramount CEO David Ellison that started at $18 a share in cash with the offer propelled ever higher by the determined Ellison.

	










			Watch on Deadline		






	Paramount offered other sweeteners as well, including a so-called ticking fee of about $7 million a day to be distributed to WBD shareholders and kicking in October 1 if the deal hadn’t closed (Paramount had to pay through Tuesday), and Larry Ellison backstopping all of the equity financing.

	Zaslav has long been among the highest-paid CEOs in media, and some years across all sectors. His package also includes a $34.2 million cash bonus, $44.2 million in perquisites, and a unspecified amount for a tax reimbursement. The cash for WBD equity payment reported today is higher than around $517 million initially anticipated.

	A spokesman for the company noted that Zaslav had doubled the number of WBD employees who held equity in merged Discovery and Warner Media to about half of the total workforce, so they’re also benefitting from the Paramount takeover.

53,2026-10-08,"Wall Street's move into blockchain technology could open a vast new market for crypto, but investors betting on tokenization may find better opportunities than bitcoin BTC$82,435.87 and ether ETH$2,493.68, according to the well-followed research firm Citrini Research.
In a 79-page report titled Breaking the Wall, published Thursday, the research firm argued that bringing stocks, bonds and other financial assets onto blockchains could create new businesses in trading, lending and payments.
Tokenization turns traditional assets into digital tokens that can move between financial platforms and potentially trade around the clock. A tokenized stock, for example, could serve as collateral for a loan directly from an investor's digital wallet, without going through a traditional brokerage.
Citrini is best known for its research on technology and markets, including artificial intelligence, and for running the most-followed Substack newsletter, with more than 263,000 followers. The firm’s research on AI went viral earlier this year, sparking widespread fear and leading to a brief market meltdown. 
The research argued that flexibility opens new markets for trading platforms, lenders, stablecoin issuers and companies that handle securities ownership records. And the biggest winners may be the companies and crypto projects collecting fees from all that activity, the report said.
“We can't assume that majors, primarily BTC and ETH, will make new ATHs on this,” the report said, referring to all-time highs. “Even if they do, there are better expressions.”
To identify opportunities, Citrini proposed two investment baskets, one focused on publicly traded stocks and another on crypto tokens.
The report was particularly interested in businesses that stand to earn fees as more of Wall Street moves onchain.
It highlighted tokenization firm Securitize (SECZ), which maintains the legal link between blockchain tokens and the securities they represent.
Crypto exchange Coinbase COIN$171.96·Market Closed and digital brokerage Robinhood HOOD$107.02·Market Closed offer exposure through their trading platforms and blockchain infrastructure, while stablecoin issuer Circle CRCL$80.85·Market Closed could benefit from greater demand for its USDC stablecoin to settle transactions, the report said.
Citrini also mentioned Figure Technology Solutions (FIGR) for tokenized lending, SoFi SOFI$15.62·Market Closed for stablecoin payments, and institutionally focused digital asset exchange operator Bullish BLSH$31.41·Market Closed, CoinDesk’s parent company, which is acquiring share registrar Equiniti.
Citrini said it was ""actually more excited"" about its separate crypto-token basket, which it said offers broader exposure than the limited universe of listed companies.
“If we’re right that stocks, commodities and other financial assets are moving onchain, then eventually all of the financial products built around those assets should follow them,” the report said.
Its analysis highlighted Aerodrome (AERO), a trading platform that could collect fees from tokenized stock transactions, and Maple SYRUP$0.2335, which manages blockchain-based lending products for institutional investors.
It also included Pendle (PENDLE), which allows investors to trade future income from interest-bearing assets, while Ondo Finance ONDO$0.4792 provides tokenized U.S. Treasury, stock products and, more recently, perpetual futures. Aave AAVE$166.03 offers lending infrastructure, Uniswap (UNI) provides a decentralized marketplace for trading, while crypto protocol Ethena (ENA) issues stablecoins and recently expanded into digital finance, combining high-yield savings, cards and payments.
Citrini's crypto basket also included ether.fi (ETHFI) for crypto-based financial services, Chainlink LINK$12.85 for market data and LayerZero ZRO$2.0495 for connecting blockchains. All three could benefit as tokenized assets spread across financial platforms and blockchain networks.
The report also highlighted Derive DRV$0.3615, a decentralized options trading protocol that could benefit if tokenized stocks and other financial assets bring more derivatives trading onto blockchains.
It also gave a nod to up-and-coming perpetual futures trading venues Lighter (LIT) and Variational (VAR). Perpetual futures, or perps, are contracts that let traders bet on an asset’s price rising or falling without owning it, and unlike traditional futures, they have no expiration date. Hyperliquid (HYPE) has emerged as a dominant blockchain-based platform for perps trading, Citrini said the two challenger venues may gain traction alongside Hyperliquid as the broader perps market grows. The report also included exposure to Hyperliquid in its stock basket via the Bitwise Hyperliquid ETF (BHYP).
The Citrini report, however, cautioned that growing trading volumes and network activity don't always translate into higher token prices. Investors need to look at how protocols make money, who collects the fees and whether token holders get a share of that.
The report also flagged liquidity spread across competing blockchains, security risks that could slow adoption, and legal hurdles of synthetic tokenized stocks that offer exposure to share prices without granting investors the voting rights or direct ownership that come with traditional shares.
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.

55,2026-10-08," by Jason Lemkin | 5 Interesting Things, Blog Posts, Scale
Cloudflare latest quarter reported of $696.1 million, up 36%, which annualizes to $2.8 billion. Three months earlier, it had cut more than 1,100 roles, about 20% of the company, and the stock dropped 23%. In Q2 growth accelerated, and the stock jumped 17% after hours. Four days later Cloudflare raised $2.5 billion at a 0% coupon.
Let’s dig into … Why.

Cloudflare closed at $355.01 on October 6, a market cap of about $126 billion. It ended September 2025 at $214.59.
The path in between:
The multiple, on market cap to trailing twelve months revenue:
 

Over the year, trailing revenue grew about 34% and the multiple expanded about 25%. Together they account for the 65%.
The quarterly growth series since Q1 2025 runs 27%, 28%, 31%, 34%, 34%, 36%. Revenue beat the $664.7 million consensus by $31 million.

The expansion numbers moved with it:
The guide is lower. Q3 is guided to $736 to $737 million, or 31% growth, and the full year to $2.864 to $2.870 billion, or 32%. By my arithmetic that implies about 29% in Q4. The CFO said the contract mix now makes quarter-to-quarter revenue harder to forecast, so guidance stays prudent.
The cut was announced May 7 alongside Q1 results. Cloudflare had 5,156 full-time employees at the end of 2025. Matthew Prince told employees internal AI usage had grown more than 600% in three months, and 97% of R&D was using AI coding tools. The memo said the move was “not a cost-cutting exercise.”

What it cost:
The first full quarter after the cut:
The internal tooling is now a product. Cloudflare OS gave finance, legal, and procurement the agent tools most companies limit to developers, wired into systems of record with audit and controls. Cloudflare open sourced it in August, and large systems integrators have asked to implement it. At Birthday Week on October 1, a waitlist opened for a fully managed version.
We run SaaStr with 3 humans and 20+ agents, so we’re biased toward believing this model works. Q2 is one quarter of evidence that it can at $2.8 billion.
Cloudflare ended Q2 with 7.4 million developers. Nearly 2 million arrived in the quarter, against 1.5 million in all of 2025. Prince said he made the team triple-check the number.

It also added more than 80,000 paying customers in the quarter, as many as it had in total at its 2019 IPO, for 74% year-over-year paying customer growth.
The source is vibe coding. Prince named Lovable, Replit, and Base44 as platforms whose generated code often deploys to Cloudflare, and said his own executive assistant now ships household tools there. Workers only charges when code is doing work, which suits agents that spin up and down.
The enterprise motion set records in the same quarter. Named deals included a five-year, $31.8 million contract with a media company, an 18-month, $15.9 million contract with a Fortune 1000 technology company, and a three-year, $11 million contract with a European Global 2000. Asked whether Cloudflare would drift back to pure PLG, Prince said that on $10 million-plus contracts, “no one’s putting it on a credit card.”
Sales and channel stayed large through the self-serve surge:
A pool of funds is a dollar commitment the customer draws down across any product. The three named this quarter:
All three are for the Workers developer platform.

The CFO described the model moving from purely ratable subscriptions toward a mix of pool of funds, consumption, and what Cloudflare calls T-shirt sizing. Customers are burning through commitments faster and renewing early, which raises the variance customer by customer.
RPO was $2.732 billion, up 38%, with current RPO up 35%. Maximum deal size has stepped up with each product line: about $1 million for application services, double-digit millions for Zero Trust, and triple digits for the developer platform.
CrowdStrike’s Falcon Flex works the same way. The cost Cloudflare accepted is a revenue number that’s harder to guide.
In November 2025, Cloudflare’s data said non-human traffic would pass human traffic in the second half of 2027. In March 2026 the estimate moved to the first half of 2027. In May 2026 it happened. Prince’s current projection is that non-human traffic could reach 1,000 times human traffic in five years, with the caveat that he has been wrong at every step.

Cloudflare is building a payments layer for that traffic. Monetization Gateway lets a customer sell any resource behind Cloudflare, such as a page, an API, a dataset, or an MCP tool. Wallets lets agents pay, and cloudflare.pay handles identity and trust.
Prince’s rough math on the scale:
The payments layer has no revenue yet. The CFO said Workers is still the fastest-growing line, followed by SASE. Blocking malicious bot traffic carries no extra charge.

For three years Cloudflare grew in the high 20s to low 30s. In Q1 2025 it was at 27%. Five quarters later it’s at 36%.
How much of the 36% is AI can’t be measured from the outside. Cloudflare doesn’t break out Workers revenue, and both Prince and the CFO declined to size it on the call. The evidence is the developer count, the traffic mix, and the named deals. The guide implies about 29% growth in Q4.

56,2026-10-08,"To most people, Anne Black is a renowned Danish ceramist. Her pared-back vases, cups, flowerpots and wall hooks, all handcrafted from porcelain, can be found in tasteful homes around the world. To clothing enjoyers of a certain taste, Anne Black is something else entirely. Tucked away in a high-ceilinged space with wooden floors on the border of Vesterbro and Frederiksberg, an area known as Copenhagen’s “Little Paris,” her eponymous store houses one of Europe’s most singular selections of independent labels. Where else would you find cult royalty like Daniela Gregis and Wommelsdorff alongside slow-fashion talents Unkruid and Tíscar Espadas?  
Black has no formal background in fashion. She studied at the Royal Danish Academy, where she became fascinated by Chinese porcelain. Soon after graduating in 1997, she started “dreaming of creating a universe where my own objects would be surrounded by my favorite things.” Black, switching to her native Danish, explains that the store has felt like a natural extension of her ceramics practice ever since it opened in 2013. “My starting point was, and still is, to look for materials that speak to my own aesthetics across my home, wardrobe and work.”
Black’s eye for materials runs through every piece on the rack at her store on Tullinsgade, a quiet street of ground-floor shops with apartments above, just around the corner from the bustling Vaernedamsvej. If fabric has become fashion’s new religion, then Anne Black is heaven on earth. Across all ten labels currently stocked, clothes are not merely made of fabric. They are the wearable expressions of it. Here, a designer’s POV begins long before silhouette or styling, with warp and weft, texture and hand-feel. 
Take minä perhonen, the Japanese label with a Finnish name that has been part of Black’s universe since day one. Founded by Akira Minagawa in 1995, it has developed thousands of artisanal textile designs characterized by hand-drawn patterns. Most iconic among them is “tambourine,” defined by circles formed from 25 embroidered dots. Or take Unkruid, the Antwerp-based label best known for reviving and refining heritage fabrics, from Austrian Himalaya loden to Alsatian kelsch. Its non-seasonal “series” of rural-inspired garments feel less like collections than chapters in an ongoing textile research project.  
This obsession with material is nowhere more explicit than at Pure Fabrications, another emerging label stocked by Anne Black and run by its buyer. Working with a loose-knit collective of artisanal and amateur weavers from Europe and Asia, it creates extremely limited batches of garments and homeware, all made in Denmark for a select group of stores. One of only three custom pieces currently in stock is a long coat with an entirely handwoven, rug-like reverse. Like every item the label creates, its shape feels designed around the material — fresh from the loom — rather than the other way around.    
“I can be very moved by the extreme care and effort that lies in every little step,” Black says about the approach of the designers she works with. When she encounters a new label, one of the first things she considers is the quality of the materials used. How does she know? “It’s crucial that it immediately speaks to my senses and that I myself want to touch and own it.” 
What matters even more, however, are the people behind the clothes. “It may sound strange,” she says, “but before anything else I notice whether I find the person behind the brand likeable.” That’s because, as she puts it, “a big part of running a store like ours is the relationships and respect for each other’s work.” It’s key to Anne Black: her store doesn’t simply stock designers, it collaborates with them. 
“We always go through every detail of the garments and dyeing processes before I start making their pieces,” says Janny Jingyi Ye of seventyfive, the London-based artisanal label rooted in Chinese aesthetics that Anne Black began stocking this past season. “I think because of their background as makers,” she adds, “they are willing to take risks. They not only listen to my ideas, but also make suggestions that really resonate with me.”
That human-first attitude extends to the people Black surrounds herself with. Her buyer, Marck Windekilde, joined the team eight years ago through one of its brands rather than through a retail background. His role at minä perhonen first brought him into Black’s orbit. They met in Copenhagen, when the Tokyo-based label organized an exhibition of textiles and interior objects at the store. Years of Paris showroom appointments and long post-market dinners gradually turned into a working relationship. “It would be hard for me to think of another store I’d want to work at,” says Windekilde, who’s also creative director of Pure Fabrications. “There’s something special about this place.” 
Others recognize that, too. Over the past ten years or so, Anne Black has built a loyal local following and become a true IYKYK spot. That growing reputation, however, hasn’t translated into more square feet. In fact, the recent move from upscale Gammel Kongevej to the cozy Tullinsgade was to a smaller, quirkier space. 
The new address invited the team to approach the store itself more freely. “It’s quite funny,” Black says, “because normally we would only open the store once it’s looking pristine and perfect.” Instead, Copenhagen-based Swedish architect Filip Ivarsson Eckerdal encouraged them to “inhabit it slowly” and “remain in an experimental phase for as long as possible,” he says. Much like the clothes on its racks, the store isn’t supposed to arrive fully formed: it will “evolve organically,” gaining character through the scuffs and marks of day-to-day use. 
Lukas Mauve is a Dutch-based writer and creator of the PRESENT FOREVER newsletter. A trained philosopher who turned to fashion, he now specializes in emerging and independent clothing brands.

60,2026-10-08,"
					By Liz Shackleton 
pmc-media-editor-2024

EXCLUSIVE: Mockingbird Pictures is launching three new titles at Busan’s Asian Contents & Film Market, heading a slate of horror, supernatural mystery, comedy and family drama as the range of genre films emerging from Vietnam and Southeast Asia continues to expand.

	Horror, the bedrock of Southeast Asian genre cinema, features prominently on the slate through two new titles: Indonesia’s The Doll Ghawiah and Vietnam’s The River Demon (Út Lan 2). However, another new title, Bluebells Studios’ The Happiest Cell, is a prison-set comedy drama revealing that Vietnamese studios are moving beyond horror to experiment with different genres.

	










	Directed by Guntur Soeharjanto, The Doll Ghawiah is the latest chapter in Hitmaker Studios’ long-running The Doll universe, a franchise that has drawn around 5 million admissions in Indonesia. Set in 1988, this new installment follows a family who leaves Jakarta for Bandung in search of a fresh start, only to be terrorized by the restless spirit of a young girl and her doll, Ghawiah.




			Watch on Deadline		






RELATED: Zhang Yimou Teases ‘Three-Body Problem’ At BIFF Opening; Jury Talks ‘The Assassin(s)’ Controversy & Competition Impact

The River Demon, directed by Tran Trong Dan, pushes Mockingbird’s slate into darker territory with an intense supernatural horror story rooted in Vietnamese folk beliefs. Following the success of The Guardian Demon, the film expands the filmmaker’s exploration of curses, rural legends and buried secrets through the story of a haunted riverside village, where the emergence of a two-headed serpent marks the beginning of a series of terrifying events.

	Directed by Nguyen Nhat Trung, The Happiest Cell features a star-studded cast headed by Tuan Tran (Leaving Mom), Ma Ran Do (Hijacked) and Luong The Thanh (Betting With Ghost). Scheduled for release in Vietnam on November 20, the film is described as a “story that finds humor, friendship and humanity inside one of society’s harshest environments.”

	










RELATED: Maggie Gyllenhaal On Telling Women’s Stories; Casting Ellen Burstyn & Dakota Johnson In ‘Flesh Impact’ – Busan

	Meanwhile, some of Mockingbird’s older titles are finding success at the box office and through international sales. Zero Meets Hero (Nghỉ Hè Sợ Nghỉ Hưu), directed by Huynh Lap, recently closed its domestic run with nearly $8 million (VND207.7BN), making it the third highest-grossing Vietnamese film of 2026. Blending comedy, family drama and supernatural elements, the film revolves around the relationship between a Gen Z grandson and his war-veteran grandfather.

Luck For Sale (Lên Hương), directed by Khuong Ngoc, is set for U.S. theatrical release October 9 across AMC, Regal and Cinemark theaters, accompanied by special screenings and a promotional tour with the film’s director and cast. The film is built around themes of family, mortality and generational wounds.

	“We are not looking for one single formula for Southeast Asian cinema,” said Phong Duong, Business Director of Mockingbird Pictures. “What excites us is the range – established franchises, box office performers, culturally rooted genre films, emotionally driven dramas and major star-led productions. Southeast Asia is producing increasingly diverse commercial cinema, and our role is to help each film find the international audience and market where it can connect most strongly.”

	In addition to international sales, Mockingbird is also a distributor of local and international films in Vietnam, handling around 60 theatrical releases and more than 100 post-theatrical titles annually.

61,2026-10-08,"
					By Matthew Carey 
Documentary Editor, Awards

International Documentary Festival Amsterdam has revealed the 47 projects selected for IDFA Forum 2026, the festival’s prestigious co-production and co-financing market.

	The final lineup, culled from almost 900 submissions, “brings together projects in development, production, and near completion, showcased across the Forum Pitches, Roundtable Presentations, Rough Cut Presentations, and IDFA DocLab Forum (new media),” IDFA notes.

	Scroll for the full lineup of projects. IDFA Forum runs November 15-18, while the festival itself unfolds from Nov. 12-22.





	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	Documentary
	



	
		
					IDFA Adds 100+ Titles To 2026 Lineup: Oscar Contenders '14th,' 'Closure', 'Once Upon A Time In Harlem' & More		
			















	Documentary
	



	
		
					Filmmaker Tatiana Huezo Named Guest Of Honor For 39th Edition Of International Documentary Festival Amsterdam		
			







	Some prominent filmmakers will be presenting projects in this year’s Forum: Wang Bing (Jiabiangou); Vitaly Mansky (Odesa.Mama); Rachel Leah Jones (Podium (You Have Three Minutes)); Liang Zhao (Ship of Fools), and Paraguayan director Juanjo Pereira with Examination, his follow up to the highly regarded Under the Flags, the Sun (2025).

	










			Watch on Deadline		






	Along with Paraguay, projects hail from around the world including Tunisia, Namibia, Myanmar, India, Kyrgyzstan, China, Taiwan, Palestine, the U.S., and Canada among others, “reflecting a broad range of geographies, production contexts, and perspectives,” IDFA observes. “Europe maintains a strong presence in the lineup, comprising major documentary markets (France) and smaller producing nations (Greece, Hungary). Gender balance has been achieved across all the formats, with a commitment to wider diversity beyond parity alone.”

	Recognizing how the documentary market has become “increasingly fragmented and harder to navigate,” IDFA is modifying the setup of the Forum “to help projects communicate more clearly the support they need and to foster their meaningful engagement with funders, broadcasters, producers, and other collaborators.”

	Under the revised structure, selected projects will be showcased either to a larger audience at the Forum Pitches or in the more intimate setting of the Roundtable Presentations, allowing for more in-depth conversations. “Both formats offer access to the Forum’s one-on-one meetings with industry professionals. Co-production and producer-to-producer meetings also remain central to Forum, with producers now invited to meet with selected projects in search of co-production partners.”

	










	Adriek van Nieuwenhuijzen, Head of IDFA Industry, said in a statement, “Despite the challenges of the market, both in terms of finance and distribution, we strongly believe that IDFA Forum should provide space for filmmakers who approach their craft with courage and curiosity. We champion works that surprise, reveal, and embrace complexity, and we hope to see all of these projects find audiences, whether on screens, big or small, via headsets, or in immersive installations.”

Forum Pitch and Roundtable Presentations

	Twenty-nine projects have been selected for the Forum Pitches and Roundtable Presentations. Mehran Tamadon’s One Step Closer grapples with the consequences of polarization in a French rural community, while Andrew H. Brown’s Wild Goose Days lenses the Indigenous KYUK radio station in Alaska, threatened by government policies. War takes center stage in Roman Bondarchuk and Vadym Ilkov’s mosaic-style Recovery, which reframes Ukraine’s reconstruction as resistance.

	Archival filmmaking is present in the selection, with Juanjo Pereira’s Examination, which draws on declassified materials and their reinterpretations to reveal power systems taming a land and jaguars. Meanwhile, Christina D. Bartson’s The Contract of the Century reckons with the legacies of colonialism, zeroing in on the energy megaproject, the Baku-Tbilisi-Ceyhan pipeline.

Rough Cut Presentations

	Six projects have been selected for this year’s Rough Cut Presentations. Featuring 20-minute excerpts, the presentations offer the buyers, festival representatives, and funders attending both IDFA Forum and Docs for Sale an early look at projects nearing completion. Vikram Singh’s Camels of the Sea offers a distinct take on climate change and endangered ways of living as illegal salt pans threaten India’s rare swimming camels. Facts join fantasy in Josefina Buschmann Mardones’ Artificial Clouds, an AI’s absorbing travelogue through Chile, tracing its ecological footprint. Nikos Pilos and Arsinoi Pilou’s Resisting Residents embeds us in an Athens squatted community, capturing solidarity as a lived practice.

IDFA DocLab Forum (new media)

	The market’s new media strand, IDFA DocLab Forum, presents 12 projects, boasting diverse formats, from an immersive installation to VR, XR, mixed reality, a participatory performance, and an interactive documentary. The projects engage with such resonant themes as digital labor, bodily memory, virtual violence, and collective participation in democracies. Katja Verheul’s Mine Land looks at the remnants of war still unearthed in a Bosnian forest 31 years on. Hyeunjoo Woo and Jiyun Park’s How to Make a Chiffon Cake proposes an intriguing scenario, turning a woman’s invisible domestic work into AI training data. Mark Mushiva and Daniel Chein’s Time Hunter XR: Colonial Protocols also appropriates tech tools for resistance, conjuring a speculative decolonial mission in 1912 Namibia.

Forum Pitch and Roundtable Presentations

	• Dr. Hedgehog, dir. Peter Alsted, prod. Birgitte Keseler / Elk Film, Katrine Dolmer / Elk Film (Denmark)

					By Liz Shackleton 
pmc-media-editor-2024

EXCLUSIVE: The creative team behind Hong Kong breakout animated feature Another World are developing a second theatrical feature together, Blacky the Metal Arm Cat, which they’re presenting to potential partners at Busan’s Asian Contents & Film Market.

	Produced by two Hong Kong animation outfits – Another World producer Point Five Creations and rising animation house Intoxic Studio – the 2D project is written and directed by veteran animator Nic Ho (Mountains and Seas) based on his acclaimed short film.

	The project is spearheaded by Point Five’s Polly Yeung, with Another World director Tommy Ng Kai-chung on board as art director. Intoxic Studio’s Ivana Lai is co-producing the film.

	










			Watch on Deadline		







	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					Vietnam's Mockingbird Launches Horror Duo & Comedy Drama 'The Happiest Cell' At Busan's ACFM		
			















	News
	



	
		
					Marvel Animation Sets Release Dates For New 'Zombies' Specials & 'Your Friendly Neighborhood Spider-Man' Season 2		
			







	The creative team describe the project as blending “high-stakes action, urban fantasy and imaginative world-building to explore identity, resilience and human purpose.”

	Set in the ancient metropolis of Golden City, the film tells the story of a legendary warrior, once known as “Golden Claw,” who suffers a fall from grace following a devastating betrayal. He is saved by an enigmatic young mechanic who equips him with a custom mechanical arm. But when the mechanic suddenly disappears, he is forced to descend into a mysterious underworld and confront an alien presence in order to bring her back.

	“Blacky is not a story about becoming a hero; it is a story about rediscovering yourself after loss,” Ho said. “We live in an era where technology and society shift rapidly – where the skills or identities we took pride in can feel obsolete overnight. This film reflects that generation-wide feeling of loss through modern, action-packed storytelling, showing what it means to find a reason to keep moving forward.”

	Yeung also guided Another World from its roots as a short film into a theatrical feature. The film, released in 2025, became Hong Kong’s highest-grossing local animated feature of all time and scooped Best Animated Feature at Taiwan’s prestigious Golden Horse Awards. Yeung will be introducing Blacky the Metal Arm Cat to potential co-producers, financiers and sales agents at ACFM.

	










Point Five Creations has also partnered with Korean webtoon studio Creative SUMM to produce a webtoon expansion of Another World, with support from Hong Kong’s CreateSmart Initiative.

	Produced by Yeung in collaboration with Creative SUMM, and with SuperString Inc producer BeeWon Lee on board as co-producer, the webtoon serves as a launchpad for a broader transmedia expansion of Another World. Tommy Ng is also working on the webtoon as a project supervisor. Yeung and Eden Bernal are co-writing the script.

	It will focus on the fan-favorite character of Dark Sky in Another World – a headless swordsman who serves as an assassin and a guardian – as Point Five Creations prepares future film and series entries in the franchise.

	CSI, which is supporting the transmedia expansion, is an initiative under Hong Kong’s Cultural and Creative Industries Development Agency.

	Led by CEO Cho Hea-seung, Creative SUMM is a leading Korean content studio known internationally for producing mega-hit webtoon The Red Sleeve, which accumulated more than 100 million views and inspired the hit MBC television drama, as well as acclaimed webtoons including Leafie, A Hen into the Wild, Beauty Water and Underdog.

63,2026-10-08,"Live-service dominates global playtime, but can it survive cancelled projects, dwindling players, and a market in decline? Or is this the beginning of the end for forever games?"
64,2026-10-08,"Patricia Clark, vp, go-to-market, Nexxen
AI has become more than the topic du jour. It’s the topic of the year — and probably the decade — in and outside the world of advertising. For agencies, AI presents particularly compelling opportunities: less manual work, faster insights and a greater ability to connect planning, activation, optimization and measurement. And as agentic AI develops, a natural language request could become a coordinated, multi-step workflow spanning different data, media and technology platforms.
It’s one of many reasons interoperability matters, as agencies shouldn’t have to choose between flexibility and innovation, nor should they be locked into a single provider’s architecture. Open standards, like model context protocol and Agent2Agent, point toward a more unified ecosystem, where an agency’s own tools and AI agents can work securely with other external DSPs. And in advertising, where campaigns already span multiple systems, data sources and supply paths, the ability to connect those systems is fast becoming an operational mandate.
Interoperability is an enabler, but it’s not a strategy. Its value depends on what an agency is trying to achieve, the level of control an agency needs and the resources an agency can dedicate to making the model work. 
There is no one-size-fits-all approach and no single right operating model. Some agencies will continue to rely on managed service for speed, scale and simplicity. Others will opt for self-service, with direct access to platform controls and tools. Some will take a hybrid approach, leveraging a tech partner’s expertise while maintaining visibility and involvement. A handful of agencies will likely even build interoperable agent ecosystems of their own. 
These various approaches are akin to deciding what to eat or where to go for dinner: hiring a personal chef, dining in a restaurant, ordering the ingredients and prepping the meal, or grabbing takeout. All come with their own benefits, downsides and costs — from control to convenience.
Because there’s no single right operating model, selecting well-suited tech partners, platforms and DSPs is essential. They should do more than just share functionality through an API. They should provide agencies with a reliable, secure and transparent foundation that agencies can use to best fit their business needs, regardless of their chosen workflow. 
The strongest partnerships will also be designed for evolution. Agencies need to be able to access a DSP’s capabilities while retaining the flexibility to adopt new tools and standards as the market matures. They need partners willing to collaborate, provide practical expertise and help determine when to build, when to buy and when to do both.
The future of both agentic AI and the advertising industry will not come down to which agency has built the most elaborate system or even become first to adopt the latest technology. It will come down to who is solving the right problems at the right pace and leveraging tech partners and platforms to do so responsibly and sustainably. 
Open ecosystems can expand agencies’ capabilities, but without the appropriate tech partnerships in place, it’s difficult to say how effective these capabilities will truly be.

OK Future cut Goodwipes’ AI spoof from six weeks to four days, exposing big savings and burnout risks.

65,2026-10-08,"A burrito with your coffee, anyone?
Chipotle stock surged on Thursday on a report from The Financial Times that Starbucks has explored a takeover of the Mexican fast-casual chain. It could not be determined if Starbucks submitted a formal offer or what the status of its plans to acquire the company is currently, the FT said.
Shares of Chipotle jumped as much as 8% on the news before paring the gain. Starbucks shares dropped as much as 6% intraday.
A merger would combine two of the largest and most iconic brands in America's casual dining scene, with Chipotle valued at around $41 billion, and Starbucks' market cap sitting at over $103 billion on Thursday.
The move would also mark Starbucks CEO Brian Niccol's takeover of his former employer. Niccol was the CEO of Chipotle from 2018 to 2024.
Chipotle and Starbucks' shares have moved in opposite directions since it was announced that Niccol would take over the coffee chain. 
Starbucks shares have risen 19% since his role was announced in August 2024. The performance has lagged the broader market, with the S&P 500 up 42% in that time, but it's outclassed peers in the space. In his tenure, the Invesco Food & Beverage ETF (PBJ) is up just 1.4%. 
Chipotle, meanwhile, has gotten crushed. The stock has plunged by 41%, struggling against headwinds including slower sales, higher input costs, and recent public health concerns, including the Cyclospora outbreak that broadly reduced foot traffic to fast-casual chains this summer.
Starbucks and Chipotle did not immediately respond to a request for comment sent by Business Insider.
