Categories A and B have traditionally segmented the market for cars into smaller, less powerful models and larger, more powerful ones, based on engine capacity and power.“However, advances in vehicle technology, particularly with the advent of EVs, have made these criteria less effective, with manufacturers increasingly able to adjust vehicle specifications to fit within existing Category A thresholds,” LTA said in a media release.This has increased competition in Category A and narrowed its price gap with Category B. Category A premiums exceeded Category B premiums three times between February and June this year.In the latest bidding exercise on Wednesday, Category A premiums closed at S$130,001 and Category B at S$130,100.
Categories A and B have traditionally segmented the market for cars into smaller, less powerful models and larger, more powerful ones, based on engine capacity and power.
“However, advances in vehicle technology, particularly with the advent of EVs, have made these criteria less effective, with manufacturers increasingly able to adjust vehicle specifications to fit within existing Category A thresholds,” LTA said in a media release.
This has increased competition in Category A and narrowed its price gap with Category B. Category A premiums exceeded Category B premiums three times between February and June this year.
In the latest bidding exercise on Wednesday, Category A premiums closed at S$130,001 and Category B at S$130,100.
“The objective of the public consultation is to seek views on how to more effectively distinguish between mass-market and higher-end cars in the COE system, while allowing COE prices to continue to be determined by market forces,” said LTA.
It comes after Transport Minister Jeffrey Siow said at the ministry’s Budget debate in March that he had asked LTA to review the issue.
The agency engaged more than 200 members of the public, academics and automotive industry representatives on their suggestions for the COE system, which allocates vehicle quotas to control traffic congestion in Singapore.
""As vehicle technology and the vehicle market continue to evolve, it is timely to review whether the current categorisation of cars remains fit-for-purpose. Through this consultation, we invite the public to share their views on the proposed changes and trade-offs involved,"" said LTA chief operating officer Lim Zhijian.
After the merger of the two categories, each car's COE price would be adjusted by a rebate or surcharge if necessary, based on the median open market value of the car model.
The open market value is the cost of a vehicle imported into Singapore as assessed by Singapore Customs, taking into account the purchase price, freight, insurance and all other sale and delivery charges for importing the car.
This value is currently used to calculate a car’s Additional Registration Fee.
The median open market value would be based on past registrations of the same model over a set period, such as the past year. The percentile into which that median falls would then determine which fee or rebate band applies to the car.
The consultation paper outlines two possible structures. A three-band structure would have a S$15,000 rebate band, a middle band with no adjustment and a S$15,000 surcharge band. 
This would be simpler to administer but adjustments between adjacent bands would be larger, LTA said.
A five-band structure would more finely differentiate car models into S$15,000 or S$7,500 rebate bands, a middle band with no adjustment, and S$7,500 or S$15,000 surcharge bands.
This would reduce the difference in treatment between comparable cars in adjacent bands, but make administration more complex, LTA said.
LTA said it would review and publish the band for each car model annually to keep buyers informed before they decide on their purchases. 
Car retailers would be able to appeal a band assignment.
LTA also sought views on whether the fee-and-rebate system should apply to COE renewals.
Asked at a media briefing how the rebate and surcharge amounts were set, LTA told reporters that this involved a judgment of what would be a meaningful price difference between mass-market and higher-end cars.
In considering the appropriate rebate and surcharge amounts, one possible reference point is the historical price gaps between Category A and Category B. This provides an indication of the difference in COE premiums that buyers of the two groups of cars have historically been willing to pay under the existing system.
Based on historical data, the general difference between Category A and B prices has been about S$20,000 to S$30,000, notwithstanding the convergence in recent years, said the agency.
Had the system applied to the 52,678 cars newly registered in 2025, about half would have received a rebate or no change, while the other half would have faced a surcharge.
LTA also sought views on whether and how open category COEs – Category E – should change if cars only have one category.
Category E COEs can be used to register any vehicle except motorcycles. Their supply comes from 10 per cent of deregistrations from categories A and B for cars, and Category C for goods vehicles and buses.
The category is meant to accommodate changes in demand for different vehicle types. As these COEs are transferable, they are also sought by buyers who urgently need to register a vehicle without waiting for the next bidding exercise.
Bidders typically use open COEs to register vehicles in the category with the highest premium. In recent years, this has been mostly Category B cars, said LTA.
If categories A and B are merged, the open category would change from registering vehicles in three categories – two for cars and one for goods vehicles and buses – to registering vehicles in two categories – one for cars and one for goods vehicles and buses.
Category E would continue to be used mainly for cars, “at the expense” of goods vehicles and buses, said LTA.
LTA said one suggestion from focus group discussions was to scrap Category E to simplify the system, but it pointed out that this would adversely impact buyers who urgently need a car.
An alternative is to keep Category E for cars only, which would preserve those buyers' options without affecting Category C supply, it said.
LTA also addressed other suggestions about the COE system raised in focus groups.
One suggestion was to give families with young children more support with car ownership. Similar calls have been made in parliament.
LTA said the key question was how to support families’ needs while preserving a COE system that is “market-based, equitable, and transparent”.
Allocating COEs by household circumstances requires judgment about whose needs should receive greater priority, said the agency. For example, more COEs for families with young children would mean fewer COEs for caregivers and multi-generational households.
Household circumstances also change over time, making it difficult to administer such a system fairly over the lifespan of a COE, said LTA.
The agency is instead working out the details of a transport grant to help offset the transport costs of large families with three or more Singaporean children, whether by taxi or public transport.
LTA also addressed the suggestion of imposing a surcharge on those who own more than one car, similar to the Additional Buyer’s Stamp Duty for residential properties.
It said multiple-car owners are not a key driver of COE demand, and a surcharge would be unlikely to significantly affect prices and difficult to administer fairly.
Last year, about 66,000, or 13 per cent, of car-owning households had more than one car, down from 87,000, or 19 per cent, in 2012.
Focus group participants also suggested creating a separate COE category for private-hire cars. But this would require quota to be taken from categories A and B, LTA said.
Too few COEs could cut the supply of private-hire cars and raise rental rates and fares, while too many could shrink the pool for private buyers and push up COE prices.
Private-hire cars are used for both ride-hail services and personal journeys, so a separate category would also need additional regulation to keep vehicles to their intended use.
Another suggestion was to ban motor dealers from bidding for COEs on behalf of their customers.
Rather than restricting who can bid, LTA said it was looking to encourage individual bidders by allowing them to make their initial bid online and reviewing the fees for making and revising bids.
The authority said it was also looking at ways to get motor dealers to publish unbundled car prices without COE to improve price transparency for individual buyers, who could compare prices and buy cars directly with their own COEs.
Some participants suggested changing the way the COE price is set to a pay-as-you-bid system.
The COE clearing price is currently set at the highest unsuccessful bid plus S$1, and all successful bidders in the category pay the same price.
In a pay-as-you-bid system, each successful bidder would pay the amount they actually bid. Participants believed this may encourage more conservative bids and lower COE prices.
LTA pointed out that COE bidding is an open auction, so bidders can observe the prevailing price and revise their bids during the exercise.
“Even if bidders were to pay what they bid, a rational bidder would always bid marginally above the prevailing price. This would be no different from the current system,” it said.
Most bids already cluster around the clearing price. About three in four successful Category A and B bids in 2025 were within 5 per cent of it. Among all bids, including unsuccessful ones, about two in three were within 5 per cent above or below it.

Pickleball has officially escaped the court. At some point in the last two years, it stopped being ironic and became a lifestyle: parking lots of suburban rec centers filled up, TikTok feeds followed, then came the celebrity investors, the professional leagues, the branded paddle drops, and the kind of mainstream saturation that tells you a sport has officially graduated from niche to undeniable. Getting turned into a Wii-style digital pastime is, at this point, a rite of passage for any sport that has truly made it. Tennis got there. Golf got there. Boxing, baseball, bowling. MegaShot Pickl+ is the moment pickleball collects that particular badge, and it does so with an actual sensor-embedded pickleball racket for authentic gameplay, not some generic rectangular controller.
The smart paddle pairs with your phone, tablet, or laptop over Bluetooth and sends the game to your TV (via an included cable) for big-screen play, mimicking your real swing movements on screen and firing haptic feedback through the handle on every hit. A split-screen mode lets you play multiplayer on the same screen too, making it a great family entertainer. No court reservation, no weather to negotiate with. The Wii taught an entire generation that swinging something in your living room could feel surprisingly real. Pickl+ is betting that a generation raised on that premise is ready to do it again, this time for the sport that has genuinely taken over the world, and without an extra console to add to the mix. The Starter Edition is on Kickstarter now for $175, down from a planned retail price of $249 and the campaign has been backed 500% in just 24 hours! Pickl+ will be compatible with sports like tennis, ping pong, and badminton with more racket sports planned for the same smart-paddle platform.
Designer: MegaShot
Click Here to Buy Now: $175 $249 (30% off). Hurry, only a few left!

Under the hood, Pickl+ is built around a 9-axis sensor that reads the angle, speed, and arc of every swing for precise motion tracking. The haptic feedback responds distinctly to different kinds of hits, making the on-screen action feel genuinely tied to what your arm is actually doing. The paddle weighs around 300 grams, carries a 2000 mAh battery rated for up to five hours of continuous play, and charges over USB-C. Bluetooth 5.2 handles the wireless pairing, while an MHL cable connects your phone to the TV when the living room demands a proper screen. The app runs on Android 14+, iOS 18+, Windows, and macOS. Setup is as minimal as it sounds: download, charge, pair, connect, play. Unlike the Wii, Switch, or the Meta Quest, no extra console or hardware needed.
Most gaming hardware asks you to adapt to the machine: learn the buttons, the gestures, the control scheme. Pickl+ goes the other direction. You know how to hold a paddle, and you know what a swing feels like. Rather than turning a conventional controller into a sports accessory, Pickl+ turns the sports object itself into the controller. The technology is essentially trying to disappear into the physical experience, which is part of what makes it a more interesting object than just a clever game controller.


Ten-plus game modes ship at launch, including Streak, Practice, Split Mode, Career, Local Multiplayer, Target, and Beach Mode, with more already in the pipeline. VIP unlocks exclusive virtual courts, daily bonus challenges, glowing paddle effects, custom ball trails, and profile cosmetics.
Before the Kickstarter campaign went live, the beta version of the paddle was showcased at New York Toy Fair, where people across different age groups, including gaming enthusiasts and industry experts, got hands-on time with the motion tracking, gameplay, etc. It gave MegaShot an early real-world read on how quickly people understood and enjoyed the experience. Motion gaming can look impressive in a controlled demo; the real test is whether someone can pick up the paddle, understand what to do, and immediately have fun. New York Toy Fair gave MegaShot that read before asking anyone to back the campaign.

MegaShot arrived at this with receipts. Before Pickl+, the company built a smart bat, which sounds hyperlocal until the numbers land: 100,000+ units sold worldwide, 2,700+ Amazon reviews, and ratings above 4.5 across Google, Amazon, the Play Store, and iOS. The Smart Bat earned the team a spot on Shark Tank India, coverage across major publications, and placement on Walmart shelves. Cricket was a demanding proving ground, the kind of sport where precision hardware lives or dies by real player feedback. That foundation gave MegaShot a validated tech stack, a battle-tested supply chain, and the track record that earns trust going into a bigger, more globally ambitious launch.
Pickleball is the entry point for something larger. The same paddle hardware is designed to eventually support tennis, badminton, table tennis, and squash, with the VIP Pass carrying across sports as they are added. That roadmap turns Pickl+ from a single-sport product into a platform for multiple racket-sport experiences, which changes the value calculation considerably. The most globally accessible racket sport of this decade is also, as it turns out, the perfect vehicle for a company that built its credibility in one market and is now ready to find out how far a smart paddle can travel.

The Starter Edition, one smart paddle and an MHL cable, is available at its $175 Kickstarter launch price, versus a planned $249 retail price. Duo packs start at $275 for two paddles with a cable, while the Starter VIP Duo Pack, which throws in a VIP Pass, runs $315.
The Signature Edition, the premium single-paddle option, comes in at $349. The Signature Duo Edition bundles two premium paddles with cables, a VIP Pass, and protective sleeves for $499. Worldwide shipping begins in early 2027, covering the US, Canada, the UK, Germany, Australia, New Zealand, UAE, and India.

92,2026-10-08,"Tokenization firm Securitize (SECZ) said it is bringing shares of Apple, Nvidia, Tesla and other major U.S. companies in the Solana (SOL) blockchain, joining a growing race to make traditional stocks available through crypto trading infrastructure.
The new product, called Securitize Stocks, will initially cover 12 companies, including Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet, Meta, Netflix, Circle, Strategy and Palantir, the firm said Thursday in a press release shared with CoinDesk.
At first, trading of those stock will be available on Securitize's existing Solana-based platform, with Jump Trading providing liquidity and transactions settling in USDC stablecoin.
The stocks are also expected to trade on the New York Stock Exchange's planned 24/7 digital trading platform and the upcoming OKXICE Tokenized Securities Venue, a joint venture between NYSE parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE) and crypto exchange OKX. Both expansions remain subject to the venues launching and meeting regulatory requirements.
The move comes as Wall Street firms are pushing to bring equities onto blockchain markets, allowing around-the-clock trading, faster settlement and potentially use of stocks as collateral in onchain lending markets.
U.S. regulators are also weighing how to accommodate these products. The Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) rolled out an “innovation exemption” last month to open a path for new tokenized securities trading venues built on blockchain rails.
Read more: SEC opens door to tokenized U.S. stock trading. Here’s who could benefit
Florida-based Securitize, founded in 2017, is one of the longest-running companies focused on bringing traditional financial assets onto blockchain rails. The firm currently issued $4.5 billion in tokenized assets, and is best known for issuing the first blockchain-based money market fund from BlackRock, the world's largest asset manager.
The firm attracted backing from investors including BlackRock and asset manager ARK Invest before it went public on the New York Stock Exchange in June. It has also tokenized some $300 million of its own publicly-traded shares, available on Solana and Avalanche.
Regarding Securitize Stocks, the company said each of the stock tokens will be backed by an actual share and preserve the benefits and shareholder rights, including dividends and voting rights. The tokens represent security entitlements rather than direct ownership on a company's shareholder register, though conversion may become available when issuers adopt tokenization.
“The opportunity is to bring equities onchain without leaving behind the ownership, investor protections and market infrastructure that make U.S. capital markets work,” said Carlos Domingo, Chairman and CEO of Securitize. ”Securitize Stocks are designed around that principle, while creating a bridge to a future where issuers themselves can participate directly in tokenization.”
RQD will support the clearing, custody and settlement infrastructure connecting the tokenized shares to traditional securities markets, while Ripple Prime plans to explore institutional uses for the assets.
Eligible investors in the U.S., Europe and other permitted markets will be able to trade Securitize Stock tokens, initially during extended market hours, with plans to expand to 24/7 access.
The OKX-ICE joint venture filed this week to introduce tokenized stock trading under the SEC's framework, another sign of growing interest from established exchanges in blockchain-based securities markets.
“Tokenized equities give investors around the world access to investments they might otherwise never have had the opportunity to participate in, at internet scale,” said Nick Ducoff, general manager of institutional at Solana Foundation.
UPDATE (Oct. 8, 14:10 UTC time): Added background about Securitize.

94,2026-10-08,"by Kyra Piperides
Canva
It’s quite likely that if you own your own sizeable home as a single person, that you’ll be interested in letting a bedroom out to someone as a way to recoup some of the costs of paying the mortgage and utilities. Because let’s be real, houses are expensive, maintaining them is expensive, and running them even more so. It’s tough enough on two incomes, but if you don’t have a partner who is paying half the bill, things can feel super pricey. And even though you have to sacrifice some of your independence, a roommate can help mitigate that.
So naturally the guy in this story decided to rent a room of his house out – and when a long-term friend was looking for a place to live, it was a no-brainer. But because he was such a good friend, he didn’t seek to make a profit out of him at all, giving the guy a great deal with low rent, and the pleasure of living together. But little did the homeowner know what a slob his friend was, and how eventually he’d be taking advantage of his friend’s generosity.
Read on to find out how.
I own a three bedroom, two bathroom house, and I have a roommate who I have been good friends with for years.
I charge him $475 per month, and he has his own room and bathroom and access to all common areas of the house whenever he wants.
That includes the basement, where I have one finished room that I decorated and set up with a futon, two couches, and an entertainment setup.
It’s used as a guest room for when we have people visit and also a social area if we have parties.
Let’s see how his roommate has started overstepping.
He is a gamer and spends all his time in his room and honestly his room is kind of trashed because he doesn’t clean it.
He just told me last night that he plans on moving his TV and Xbox into the basement room, and claims it is for my benefit in case he is too loud playing COD in his room when I’m trying to sleep.
I told him no because that is essentially making that social room his second bedroom.
I said he might as well pay me twice the rent if he plans on taking over another room, because I know how much time he spends on his Xbox and I don’t want that room to be his “man cave” or whatever.
Read on to find out how this guy is feeling about this.
For the rent I charge him, he is already getting a great deal that barely covers his share of the bills so I am not even getting much of a profit.
Not to mention I already end up cleaning up the basement room myself after he has people over because he doesn’t do it.
So am I wrong for refusing?
AITA?
Given the low amount of rent his friend is paying, he has no business making these demands.
He is a tenant living in this guy’s house. That means his stuff needs to stay in his room.
He can’t just take over another room of the house like this.
Let’s see what folks on Reddit made of this.
Sign up to get our BEST stories of the week straight to your inbox.
This person thought his ‘friend’ was taking him for granted.

While others thought it was time to get rid.

If you enjoyed this story, check out this post about an artist who made sure to leave the walls of her home blank and boring before moving out, even though they were covered with gorgeous murals before then.
Meanwhile, this Redditor thought his ‘friend’ seemed incredible immature.

His friend seemingly doesn’t understand what a great deal he’s got. His rent is super low for everything he’s getting out of the situation, and he gets to live with his buddy at the same time. And that buddy is working hard to ensure that the house is super nice for himself, his roommate, and their guests – all with little to no help from his roommate who is supposed to be a friend. Really, all the roommate does is make a mess, so why on earth would he want him to move his stuff into another room of the house? Doing so would just be accepting that the basement was going to become a massive mess too.
The roommate needs a big reality check. Because even though he seems to think he owns the place, this is not his house, it’s merely the place where he rents a room. And he needs to figure that out quick, otherwise he’s going to find that he doesn’t rent a room there any more either. Then, when he tries to rent somewhere else and it costs significantly more, he’s going to realise what a great friend this guy was to him – yes, even if he didn’t let him move his Xbox downstairs.

96,2026-10-08,"By AdExchanger
Television Tunnel Vision
Amazon’s way into TV advertising ran through its Fire TV stick business and Amazon Prime Video once the program launched ads.
Its focus was on owning media properties, including the TV and movie studio, that would produce first-party ad inventory opportunities. Twitch was another such example of an owned-and-operated video ad supply channel. 
Walmart has big plans to foray into the TV ad market. But its entry points are via the Vizio smart TV manufacturing business ($2.3 billion) and streaming video ad tech startup Vibe.co ($1.4 billion), as Adweek reports.
Vizio generates some first-party ad inventory, but what those companies bring, Vibe in particular, are large bases of advertisers that aren’t endemic to Walmart (as in, they sell products or services that aren’t available at Walmart). 
Vibe has thousands of small brand or local service advertisers who are eager for CTV and don’t sell in Walmart. What they’re going to find, though, Walmart Connect GM Ryan Mayward told an Advertising Week crowd this week, is that “adding our data into their optimization capabilities is going to prove that commerce insights will drive stronger outcomes for brands, whether they sell products at Walmart or not.”
Butt At What Cost?
Amazon doesn’t just know your clothing size; it knows the shape of your butt.
And this is one of the moments where “right person, right time” could actually do more harm than good.
Getting ads for highly specific products – say, jeans designed to accentuate one’s derriere – can go one of two ways: The retargeting or personalization can generate a sale, or someone feels weirded out.
Amazon users no longer have to wonder. Since May, Amazon has been beta testing a new “About You” page that allows users to see assumptions that Amazon has about their behavior and interests based on data it’s collected, Fast Company reports.
The assumptions include everything from hobbies (like reading contemporary fiction), physical attributes (like the aforementioned flat buttocks, which was categorized as a “product preference”) and sometimes oddly specific interests (case in point: muscular brunettes?).
The new “About You” page was, for many, the gateway to understanding just how much personal data Amazon collects on its users. Amazon’s privacy guidelines state that, in addition to search and purchase data, the company also tracks everything from user reviews to conversations with Alexa to connections to third-party businesses.
Demonizing Demonetization
A few years ago, no brand wanted to be caught monetizing online disinformation. 
Now, though, advertisers are avoiding being associated with efforts to crack down on disinfo. And that aversion has spread from commercial brands to world governments, as US leaders continue to politicize anti-disinformation.
Multiple countries have withdrawn their sponsorship of #Disinfo2026, a European conference on countering disinformation, amid pressure from the Trump administration, The Guardian reports.
Lithuania’s and Canada’s foreign ministries, as well as the European External Action Service, pulled their logos from #Disinfo2026’s official conference materials. The logos were hastily taped over, suggesting a last-minute decision.
The Trump administration took issue with an event panel in which three American speakers, including a former CIA officer, were set to discuss the US as a source of “foreign information manipulation and interference” in the EU.
It’s the latest example of the US-based pushback against collective attempts to combat online disinformation. 
You might recall House Republicans holding hearings suggesting the ad industry’s demonetization of disinformation was actually meant to suppress conservative speech. Or the State Department sanctioning EU anti-disinformation leaders. Or the US government approving Omnicom’s acquisition of IPG on the condition that the agency not make brand safety decisions concerning political speech. 
No wonder the ad industry doesn’t talk about demonetizing fake news as much as it used to.
But Wait! There’s More! 
What’s up with the AppLovin/Unity animosity? [Digiday]
Meta is rolling out new AI tools for detecting ads and accounts that direct people to child sexual abuse material. [TechCrunch]
In other Meta news, a Texas gay bar blames Meta’s revamped content moderation for its social accounts being deactivated. It’s part of a larger trend of queer bars and nightclubs disappearing from Facebook and Instagram. [Chron]
Even the supposedly teen-friendly version of ChatGPT may not be all that safe for minors. [Axios]
McDonald’s has been hit with a federal lawsuit over its AI-driven pricing tool that sometimes sets markedly different prices for the same menu items across franchises. [AP News]
Branded raves are the new live events marketing craze. [WSJ]
A new site-blocking bill has been introduced to Congress that would give copyright owners the ability to block Americans’ access to foreign sites. It specifically targets VPNs. [Electronic Frontier Foundation]
Against the backdrop of Advertising Week New York this week, the programmatic ecosystem has been buzzing madly like the inhabitants of an overturned beehive after the release of Apple’s iOS 27. 
Given how much agentic AI pervades nearly every conversation these days, it’s easy to overlook how nascent the technology still is, particularly when it comes to buying ads on connected TV.
OAAA COO Patrick Dolan explains how the organization is changing to guide the out-of-home channel through programmatic’s agentic era.
PubX has acquired media-quality startup Compliant and raised a $5 million Series A as it expands its agentic ad-buying platform into the US.
Warner Bros. Discovery has a new white paper analyzing the data and methodologies of five top video measurement providers: VideoAmp, iSpot, Comscore, Innovid and Samba.
Google and the Department of Justice filed the next round of paperwork that will determine what Google’s punishment will look like in the ad tech antitrust case.

No Readers' Pick yet.
Football season is back. Understandably, sports enthusiasts throughout the United States are emptying their phones of all their betting apps in September, and it’s a little different this year. The evolving digital environment is yet another illustration of how commonly used experiences can transform in a world that is becoming increasingly mobile, with a space that was previously associated with a physical location evolving into one that is increasingly found on mobile devices. So the old-school sportsbook apps face off against Prediction Markets offering sports-related event contracts. When viewed from a home screen, the two can be virtually indistinguishable.
Table of Contents
A sportsbook app lets you place a wager at fixed odds against the operator, under rules set by a state gaming regulator. Event contracts are closer to trading, where you buy a yes-or-no position on an outcome from other users on an exchange overseen at the federal level. Their numbers aren’t directly comparable either. A sportsbook’s handle is the total amount wagered, whereas prediction markets report trading volume on an exchange.
Sportsbook apps outside the U.S. follow the first model. Betway Zambia, for example, offers one app for Android and iPhone that combines its sportsbook with casino and live casino games, and the Betway app download page on its site walks new users through installation on each device. It’s the traditional model in its plainest form, with the operator setting odds you can see before any money changes hands.
$29.5 billion. That’s how much the American Gaming Association estimated on September 4 that Americans will legally wager on the 2026 NFL season through regulated sportsbooks. Last season’s total was $29.4 billion. AGA President and CEO Bill Miller blamed prediction markets directly, saying “the growth of legal handle has stalled.”
When a market stops expanding, operators can’t count on a flood of new sign-ups each fall. The fight moves to holding on to the customers they already have. And the app becomes the thing people judge. Load speed, bet slip layout, how fast live odds refresh, and how painless a withdrawal feels on a Sunday night all feed into which icon survives the next phone cleanout. Nobody’s patient with a spinning wheel when there’s two minutes left in the fourth quarter.
Traditional sportsbooks held about 76% of legal U.S. sports wagering in 2023, but that slice dropped to 64% in the first half of 2026, based on Sensor Tower research reported by Axios. Prediction markets jumped from 1% to 16% over the same period.
DraftKings and FanDuel now operate prediction market products of their own, and Fanatics does too, which muddies things further. One familiar logo can front two legally separate products, creating an unexpected layer of complexity behind the simple interfaces people see on their phones. It is a reminder that modern technology can make complicated systems look remarkably straightforward to the average user.
Still, the frameworks aren’t interchangeable. A state-regulated sportsbook follows that state’s rules on age limits and geolocation, and it pays state gaming tax. Event contracts trade on exchanges registered with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), and the platforms offering them argue state gambling laws don’t apply to them. The AGA rejects this argument outright. It estimates users aged 18 to 20 accounted for roughly $5.1 billion of Kalshi’s volume, even though that age group can’t bet at a regulated sportsbook in 35 of the 40 U.S. jurisdictions where sports betting is legal.
On September 17, a fresh batch of self-certified sports event contracts appeared in CFTC filings. One asked if the U.S. Team would win the 2026 Presidents Cup at Medinah Country Club. Another covered the Duke Blue Devils winning the 2027 NCAA Tournament Championship. Self-certification means the exchange certifies to the regulator that a contract complies with the rules, and it can list for trading before the Commission reviews it. Sportsbooks don’t work like that; they answer to each state’s gaming rules instead.
This is key for the practical side of things. Dispute handling, rules for postponed games, age checks, and where you turn if a payout stalls all depend on which regulator oversees the product. Novig CEO Jacob Fortinsky told Axios that sports markets are now available in all 50 states and look like a permanent fixture. He may be right. But nationwide availability doesn’t settle who protects you when a bet goes sideways.
If you’re setting up a new phone this month (the iPhone 18 Pro and iOS 27 arrived on September 18), you’ll be reinstalling apps anyway. It’s a good moment to check a few basics before tapping install. Start with the product type. An app’s description or terms page should state plainly if it’s a sportsbook or an event-contract exchange, and if it doesn’t, that alone is reason to hold off on a deposit.
Location comes next. State-regulated sportsbook apps use geolocation to confirm you’re inside a legal state before accepting a bet, so an app that works on your couch may lock you out a few miles away across a state line. Betway Zambia’s app is aimed at customers in Zambia, so where you are counts there too. Then check who regulates it: a state gaming regulator for sportsbooks, the CFTC for futures contracts.
Functionality comes last. All of the above are fair game, including the following items: live betting menus, cash-out options, cleanliness of your bet history, and the location of the deposit limits and time-out tools within account settings. Before any sign-up promotion draws your attention, you need to seek out those tools that assist with responsible gambling. These are relatively simple menus, but the digital system, like a robot that is behind them is far from simple and demonstrates how advanced technology can make intricate controls and security systems simple yet accessible to control via the phone.

BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
The Business
Topic:Stock Market
The Firmus Technologies site in St Leonards, Launceston. Its data centres require a lot of energy and expansion plans have been described as ambitious. (Supplied: Tasmanian government)
The $44 billion Australian float of data centre operator Firmus appears to be in trouble.
But Firmus is not currently listed on the ASX's ""upcoming floats and listings"" webpage, and analysts who spoke to the ABC have raised concerns over the firm’s debts and the wild increase in its valuation over a short period of time.
The company is scheduled to float on the ASX on October 23.
It was touted as the biggest float since Telstra in the 1990s, but the share market debut of data centre operator Firmus is in trouble before it has even hit the share market.
Investment banks stopped taking bids for the initial public offering (IPO) today and will now determine at what price the company lists on the Australian Securities Exchange (ASX).
But Firmus is not currently listed on the ASX's ""upcoming floats and listings"" webpage, and analysts who spoke to the ABC have raised concerns over the firm's debts and the wild increase in its valuation over a short period of time.
Adding to concerns, Goldman Sachs was reportedly tapped to rescue the float from collapse at midday today.
The ABC has contacted several industry players directly connected with Firmus's fundraising, including Wilson Asset Management, Bank of America and Goldman Sachs.
The investment bank told the ABC it had no comment.
Fund manager Roger Montgomery said there had been inconsistencies in the messages given to market participants about the capital raising.
""Investors were previously told that indicative orders were already above the size of the offer at $11 a share,"" he said.
Indicative orders are what investors suggest to brokers they are willing to pay for a stock before orders are formally taking for a public listing.
""If that were true, why has the price been cut to get the deal away?"" Mr Montgomery said.
""Warren Buffett said you want to invest with people who are energetic, intelligent and honest.""
The ASX told the ABC it does not comment on individual companies.
The floats and listings webpage, however, makes it clear that ""it only records new listings for which ASX has received a formal listing application"".
""A formal application would typically only be made late in the listing process when the entity has made all necessary preparations to list in the near term (i.e. within four to six weeks),"" it says.
This would suggest a formal application has not been made by Firmus to the ASX within 15 days of its expected listing.
The caveat to this is that the ASX webpage also states that is ""usually updated within three business days of receiving a formal application"", meaning the official application may have been lodged.
Firmus was set to hit the boards of the ASX at $11 per share, but reports suggest that price may have been set too high.
Market participants have been underwhelmed by the float's roadshow, with some criticising it for having an ""unprecedented lack of detail"" and others concerned about its debt and energy needs.
The float, or total number of shares on issue, is reportedly having to be revalued down because of the lacklustre interest, with reports it could drop to about $8 per share.
Late on Wednesday, the company's representatives also pulled out of an appearance before a parliamentary inquiry into artificial intelligence today, with reports the troubled IPO was the reason for the no-show.
Data Centre operator about to float on Australian share market.
Firmus is a so-called neocloud technology company.
It builds, owns and operates upscale data centres or so-called ""AI factories"", used to run and train artificial intelligence models.
The firm is raising cash from investors to build an AI factory in Launceston and plans for two more in Tasmania.
But its ambitions extend across Australia and into South-East Asian markets as well.
Reports suggest Firmus intends to raise up to $7 billion from institutional and retail investors in its IPO, which could value the company at more than $50 billion.
But the conclusion of a $73 billion partnership with data centre firm CDC and community backlash for proposed suburban data centres has caused concern for investors.
Firmus co-founder Oliver Curtis was also jailed for 12 months in 2016 for a 2007 insider trading scheme.
Ten Cap portfolio manager Jun Bei Liu said she was steering clear of the Firmus IPO, concerned about what she said was an ""unprecedented lack of detail"" being disclosed and enormous structural risks.
""Firmus is probably one of the most polarising IPOs I have ever seen,"" she observed.
She said 97 per cent of the data centre capacity Firmus had promised had not been built.
""They might deliver cheaper earnings in two years if they build what they promise, but that's speculative,"" she said.
Ten Cap's Jun Bei Liu says she won't be taking up the Firmus IPO. (ABC News: John Gunn)
""We much prefer to be in the companies that actually build those data centres — the engineers, the electrical contractors.
Morningstar senior market strategist Lochlan Halloway said he saw ""the hallmarks of the boom phase … if not outright euphoria"" because of Firmus's wild increase in valuation in such a short period of time.
The company's valuation surged from about $6.9 billion in April and is now at $15.5 billion.
Mr Halloway was particularly concerned with the company's debt.
""The neoclouds are heavily geared. The model is to borrow against customer contracts to buy chips, then use the rent to repay the loans,"" he said in an economic note.
Lochlan Halloway says he's not written Firmus off entirely. (ABC News)
Once its data centres are built, Firmus expects to be carrying about US$30 billion of debt, around six times the US$5 billion of operating earnings it forecasts for 2028.
""Borrowed money alone does not make a bubble, and plenty of infrastructure is sensibly funded with debt. But credit is the common thread running through essentially every boom and bust cycle,"" he wrote.
But he did not write the company off completely.
Accela Research published one of the first comprehensive reports on data centres in January this year.
Its lead data centre analyst Naomi Wagura described the company has having ""ambitious growth plans"" that could ""significantly reshape electricity infrastructure"" in areas where its data centres would locate.
AI data centre developer Firmus Technologies is seeking investors ahead of a multi-billion-dollar share market float next month.
In Tasmania, Firmus's three planned data centres would make it the state's largest energy consumer, needing some 444 megawatts if they went ahead, and would crucially require the island state to add extra generation.
And while there were clear potential benefits to locals, she cautioned there also needed to be ""clear disclosure and enforceable obligations"".
The company is scheduled to float on the ASX on October 23.
